Not so much restless. Like recently, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels weirdly difficult, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music. And I’ll notice the room more than I should—the tape marks on the floor, someone’s water bottle by the mirror—and then I’m like, why am I still standing here? I was just trying to get my notebook.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like dancing or other activities, don’t bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, dancing is complicated because it’s work and it’s also the thing that used to make everything make sense. Some days I’ll still put on music and think, okay, maybe this will click, and then I get distracted by, like, the angle of the mirrors or whether the speaker is buzzing—there’s this one speaker at the studio that always cuts out on low bass, which drives me crazy.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say it brings no satisfaction. It’s just harder to tell what I’m feeling about it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or make decisions lately, like during rehearsals or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’ll sit down meaning to do one thing—like finish a grant report—and then it’s like my attention just gets yanked sideways. A truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking about a completely different piece I should make, or reorganizing a rehearsal plan, or… I don’t know, following some thread that wasn’t the task at all.\n\nIt’s not that I decide to stop focusing. It just happens, and then I look up and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty focusing have been present every day, more days than not, for the past year, or has there been more of an on-and-off pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s been… not the whole year, no. I think the low part has been around nine months. Maybe a little hard to pin down exactly, because time gets weird when I’m in rehearsal cycles—like everything is counted by performances or deadlines instead of months—but, yeah, about nine months that it’s been hanging around.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t really track it like a diet thing or anything. When I was more, um, buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals and not notice until later. Like I’d realize I’d had coffee and maybe half a banana, and then somehow it was midnight.

Yes. I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.

I keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I keep thinking about this one rehearsal space I used to love, the old floorboards and everything, and now even imagining it just feels… empty.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have you had similar periods of sadness before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The sad part, I mean. It kind of crept in and then just stayed. But yes, roughly nine months of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or felt restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. Not so much restless. Like recently, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels weirdly difficult, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music. I was just trying to get my notebook.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like dancing or other activities, don’t bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, dancing is complicated because it’s work and it’s also the thing that used to make everything make sense. Some days I’ll still put on music and think, okay, maybe this will click, and then I get distracted by, like, the angle of the mirrors or whether the speaker is buzzing—there’s this one speaker at the studio that always cuts out on low bass, which drives me crazy.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say it brings no satisfaction. It’s just harder to tell what I’m feeling about it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or make decisions lately, like during rehearsals or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.

I keep thinking about this one rehearsal space I used to love, the old floorboards and everything, and now even imagining it just feels… empty.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have you had similar periods of sadness before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: Even with the injury, I couldn’t stop moving from task to task—drawing maps, emailing collaborators, reorganizing music files, and trying to redesign the whole project in one rush.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly believed I could turn this setback into a career-defining work that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, and I was shocked when others didn’t immediately see it.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My collaborators told me I was talking so fast in meetings that they couldn’t get a word in, and I remember feeling like if I paused the whole idea would disappear.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’d sit down to finish a grant report and suddenly the sound of a truck outside, a photo on the wall, or one phrase in an old rehearsal note would pull me into a completely different plan.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started contacting presenters, dancers I barely knew, and old professors with long proposals, as if I had to build an entire new network for the piece right that minute.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: Those mountain messages and the humming voice only happened during the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. Since my mood dropped, I still feel guilty and slowed down, but I haven’t heard the voice or believed the signs were directing me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I fell behind on teaching prep and missed a grant deadline, and my studio got so chaotic that collaborators started handling basic scheduling for me. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole pattern started after my ankle injury and has been going on for about two months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed part has been present for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past several months—like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t really track it like a diet thing or anything. When I was more, um, buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals and not notice until later. Like I’d realize I’d had coffee and maybe half a banana, and then somehow it was midnight.\n\nAnd now it’s different but kind of the same result—food just tastes like cardboard a lot of the time. Even things I usually like. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging looser, which at first I thought was maybe just the fabric stretching out, because the elastic on dancewear is so unreliable, but… no, I think I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough, even when others seem satisfied with your work or efforts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community that was supposed to be there. People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.

There’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.

So… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.

I was alone in my apartment with the windows closed.\n- key: S026\n  name: Weight_Appetite_Change\n  description: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.\n  manifestation: When I was buzzing with ideas I’d skip meals without noticing, and now food tastes like cardboard, so my rehearsal clothes have been hanging looser than usual.\n- key: S027\n  name: Sleep_Disturbance\n  description: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.\n  manifestation: My sleep has been all over the place—some nights I stayed up sketching formations until dawn, and lately I fall asleep exhausted but wake at four or five with my stomach tight.\n- key: S028\n  name: Psychomotor_Changes\n  description: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.\n  manifestation: Recently even crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels slowed down, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of music.\n- key: S029\n  name: Fatigue\n  description: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.\n  manifestation: I feel wiped out after small things, like answering one email to a dancer or showering with this ankle brace on, and lying down doesn’t really recharge me.\n- key: S030\n  name: Worthlessness_Guilt\n  description: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).\n  manifestation: I keep replaying the cancelled performances and telling myself I let the dancers, the funders, and the whole community down, even though people have been kinder about it than I deserve.\n- key: S033\n  name: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\n  description: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.\n  manifestation: There were stretches when I felt dazzlingly alive and then suddenly furious if anyone questioned the plan or reminded me my ankle still needed rest.\n- key: S034\n  name: Increased_Activity\n  description: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.\n  manifestation: Even with the injury, I couldn’t stop moving from task to task—drawing maps, emailing collaborators, reorganizing music files, and trying to redesign the whole project in one rush.\n- key: S035\n  name: Inflated_Self_Esteem\n  description: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).\n  manifestation: I honestly believed I could turn this setback into a career-defining work that would change how people think about dance and wilderness, and I was shocked when others didn’t immediately see it.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My collaborators told me I was talking so fast in meetings that they couldn’t get a word in, and I remember feeling like if I paused the whole idea would disappear.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’d sit down to finish a grant report and suddenly the sound of a truck outside, a photo on the wall, or one phrase in an old rehearsal note would pull me into a completely different plan.\n- key: S040\n  name: presenters, dancers I barely knew, and old professors with long proposals, as if I had to build an entire new network for the piece right that minute.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: Those mountain messages and the humming voice only happened during the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole pattern started after my ankle injury and has been going on for about two months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed part has been present for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough, even when others seem satisfied with your work or efforts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community that was supposed to be there. People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.\n\nThere’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.\n\nSo… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. I mean, with dancing, it’s hard to separate “pleasure” from habit and responsibility, because I’ve been doing it so long that even brushing my teeth feels less automatic than warming up sometimes.

There are moments where I still notice things—like a phrase of music or someone’s weight shift—and part of me responds to it. But whether that’s enjoyment or just my dancer brain doing what it always does, I don’t know. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.

So… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. It’s more muted, I guess.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic, but I’m a dancer, so that’s kind of how my brain translates things. I keep thinking about this one rehearsal space I used to love, the old floorboards and everything, and now even imagining it just feels… empty.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have you had similar periods of sadness before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The sad part, I mean. It kind of crept in and then just stayed. I keep trying to mark it by rehearsals or seasons—like, “Was that before the spring showcase?”—because dancers remember time by performances, not calendars, you know? But yes, roughly nine months of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or felt restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. Not so much restless. Like recently, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels weirdly difficult, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music. And I’ll notice the room more than I should—the tape marks on the floor, someone’s water bottle by the mirror—and then I’m like, why am I still standing here? I was just trying to get my notebook.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like dancing or other activities, don’t bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, dancing is complicated because it’s work and it’s also the thing that used to make everything make sense. Some days I’ll still put on music and think, okay, maybe this will click, and then I get distracted by, like, the angle of the mirrors or whether the speaker is buzzing—there’s this one speaker at the studio that always cuts out on low bass, which drives me crazy.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say it brings no satisfaction. It’s just harder to tell what I’m feeling about it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or make decisions lately, like during rehearsals or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’ll sit down meaning to do one thing—like finish a grant report—and then it’s like my attention just gets yanked sideways. A truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking about a completely different piece I should make, or reorganizing a rehearsal plan, or… I don’t know, following some thread that wasn’t the task at all.\n\nIt’s not that I decide to stop focusing. I mean, I didn’t really track it like a diet thing or anything. When I was more, um, buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals and not notice until later. Like I’d realize I’d had coffee and maybe half a banana, and then somehow it was midnight.\n\nAnd now it’s different but kind of the same result—food just tastes like cardboard a lot of the time. Even things I usually like. Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.

But yes, roughly nine months of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or felt restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. Not so much restless. Like recently, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels weirdly difficult, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music. And I’ll notice the room more than I should—the tape marks on the floor, someone’s water bottle by the mirror—and then I’m like, why am I still standing here? I was just trying to get my notebook.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like dancing or other activities, don’t bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, dancing is complicated because it’s work and it’s also the thing that used to make everything make sense. Some days I’ll still put on music and think, okay, maybe this will click, and then I get distracted by, like, the angle of the mirrors or whether the speaker is buzzing—there’s this one speaker at the studio that always cuts out on low bass, which drives me crazy.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say it brings no satisfaction. It’s just harder to tell what I’m feeling about it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or make decisions lately, like during rehearsals or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’ll sit down meaning to do one thing—like finish a grant report—and then it’s like my attention just gets yanked sideways. A truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking about a completely different piece I should make, or reorganizing a rehearsal plan, or… I don’t know, following some thread that wasn’t the task at all.\n\nIt’s not that I decide to stop focusing. It just happens, and then I look up and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty focusing have been present every day, more days than not, for the past year, or has there been more of an on-and-off pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s been… not the whole year, no. I think the low part has been around nine months. Maybe a little hard to pin down exactly, because time gets weird when I’m in rehearsal cycles—like everything is counted by performances or deadlines instead of months—but, yeah, about nine months that it’s been hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past several months—like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t really track it like a diet thing or anything. When I was more, um, buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals and not notice until later. Like I’d realize I’d had coffee and maybe half a banana, and then somehow it was midnight.\n\nAnd now it’s different but kind of the same result—food just tastes like cardboard a lot of the time. Even things I usually like. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging looser, which at first I thought was maybe just the fabric stretching out, because the elastic on dancewear is so unreliable, but… no, I think I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough, even when others seem satisfied with your work or efforts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community that was supposed to be there. People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.\n\nThere’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.\n\nSo… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. I mean, with dancing, it’s hard to separate “pleasure” from habit and responsibility, because I’ve been doing it so long that even brushing my teeth feels less automatic than warming up sometimes.\n\nThere are moments where I still notice things—like a phrase of music or someone’s weight shift—and part of me responds to it. But whether that’s enjoyment or just my dancer brain doing what it always does, I don’t know. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.\n\nOr showering, especially with this ankle brace, becomes this whole production. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.\n\nThere’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.\n\nSo… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. I mean, with dancing, it’s hard to separate “pleasure” from habit and responsibility, because I’ve been doing it so long that even brushing my teeth feels less automatic than warming up sometimes.\n\nThere are moments where I still notice things—like a phrase of music or someone’s weight shift—and part of me responds to it. But whether that’s enjoyment or just my dancer brain doing what it always does, I don’t know. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.\n\nOr showering, especially with this ankle brace, becomes this whole production. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m circling the same answer, sorry. I don’t know that I can call it a clean “yes.” Dancing is… complicated. It’s work, it’s identity, it’s like asking if I’ve lost interest in having knees—well, bad example, because my knees have opinions.\n\nI still notice movement. I still think about spacing, timing, why someone’s arm looks honest in one version and decorative in another. So there’s some thread still attached. But the pleasure part is harder to find sometimes. It’s not like it used to be where I’d hear music and feel this immediate pull.\n\nOther activities… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t been paying as much attention. I can tell you dancing feels muted, but I’m not sure what box that goes in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, especially with decisions or trying to finish something that should be straightforward. Like I’ll sit down to work on a grant report, and then a truck goes by outside and I’m listening to that, and somehow I’m thinking about a loading dock at a theater I worked in years ago.

Or I’ll notice a photo on the wall, or one sentence in an old rehearsal note, and suddenly I’m making a completely different plan in my head—like a new piece, or reorganizing the whole rehearsal schedule—when I was supposed to be doing the report. It’s not that I can’t think at all. It’s more that my mind keeps grabbing the next shiny thread before I’ve finished the one in my hand.

    "role": "system",

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic, but I’m a dancer, so that’s kind of how my brain translates things. I keep thinking about this one rehearsal space I used to love, the old floorboards and everything, and now even imagining it just feels… empty.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have you had similar periods of sadness before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The sad part, I mean. It kind of crept in and then just stayed. I keep trying to mark it by rehearsals or seasons—like, “Was that before the spring showcase?”—because dancers remember time by performances, not calendars, you know? But yes, roughly nine months of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or felt restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. Not so much restless. Like recently, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels weirdly difficult, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music. And I’ll notice the room more than I should—the tape marks on the floor, someone’s water bottle by the mirror—and then I’m like, why am I still standing here? I was just trying to get my notebook.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like dancing or other activities, don’t bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, dancing is complicated because it’s work and it’s also the thing that used to make everything make sense. Some days I’ll still put on music and think, okay, maybe this will click, and then I get distracted by, like, the angle of the mirrors or whether the speaker is buzzing—there’s this one speaker at the studio that always cuts out on low bass, which drives me crazy.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say it brings no satisfaction. It’s just harder to tell what I’m feeling about it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or make decisions lately, like during rehearsals or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’ll sit down meaning to do one thing—like finish a grant report—and then it’s like my attention just gets yanked sideways. A truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking about a completely different piece I should make, or reorganizing a rehearsal plan, or… I don’t know, following some thread that wasn’t the task at all.\n\nIt’s not that I decide to stop focusing. It just happens, and then I look up and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty focusing have been present every day, more days than not, for the past year, or has there been more of an on-and-off pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s been… not the whole year, no. I think the low part has been around nine months. Maybe a little hard to pin down exactly, because time gets weird when I’m in rehearsal cycles—like everything is counted by performances or deadlines instead of months—but, yeah, about nine months that it’s been hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past several months—like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t really track it like a diet thing or anything. When I was more, um, buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals and not notice until later. Like I’d realize I’d had coffee and maybe half a banana, and then somehow it was midnight.\n\nAnd now it’s different but kind of the same result—food just tastes like cardboard a lot of the time. Even things I usually like. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging looser, which at first I thought was maybe just the fabric stretching out, because the elastic on dancewear is so unreliable, but… no, I think I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough, even when others seem satisfied with your work or efforts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community that was supposed to be there. People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.\n\nThere’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.\n\nSo… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. I mean, with dancing, it’s hard to separate “pleasure” from habit and responsibility, because I’ve been doing it so long that even brushing my teeth feels less automatic than warming up sometimes.\n\nThere are moments where I still notice things—like a phrase of music or someone’s weight shift—and part of me responds to it. But whether that’s enjoyment or just my dancer brain doing what it always does, I don’t know. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.\n\nOr showering, especially with this ankle brace, becomes this whole production. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m circling the same answer, sorry. I don’t know that I can call it a clean “yes.” Dancing is… complicated. It’s work, it’s identity, it’s like asking if I’ve lost interest in having knees—well, bad example, because my knees have opinions.\n\nI still notice movement. I still think about spacing, timing, why someone’s arm looks honest in one version and decorative in another. So there’s some thread still attached. But the pleasure part is harder to find sometimes. It’s not like it used to be where I’d hear music and feel this immediate pull.\n\nOther activities… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t been paying as much attention. I can tell you dancing feels muted, but I’m not sure what box that goes in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions lately, even for small things?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with decisions or trying to finish something that should be straightforward. Like I’ll sit down to work on a grant report, and then a truck goes by outside and I’m listening to that, and somehow I’m thinking about a loading dock at a theater I worked in years ago.\n\nOr I’ll notice a photo on the wall, or one sentence in an old rehearsal note, and suddenly I’m making a completely different plan in my head—like a new piece, or reorganizing the whole rehearsal schedule—when I was supposed to be doing the report. It’s not that I can’t think at all. It’s more that my mind keeps grabbing the next shiny thread before I’ve finished the one in my hand.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: My collaborators told me I was talking so fast in meetings that they couldn’t get a word in, and I remember feeling like if I paused the whole idea would disappear.\n- key: S039\n  name: Manic_Distractibility\n  description: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.\n  manifestation: I’d sit down to finish a grant report and suddenly the sound of a truck outside, a photo on the wall, or one phrase in an old rehearsal note would pull me into a completely different plan.\n- key: S040\n  name: Increased_Goal_Activity\n  description: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.\n  manifestation: I started contacting presenters, dancers I barely knew, and old professors with long proposals, as if I had to build an entire new network for the piece right that minute.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur exclusively during the manic episode\n  manifestation: Those mountain messages and the humming voice only happened during the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. Since my mood dropped, I still feel guilty and slowed down, but I haven’t heard the voice or believed the signs were directing me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I fell behind on teaching prep and missed a grant deadline, and my studio got so chaotic that collaborators started handling basic scheduling for me. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole pattern started after my ankle injury and has been going on for about two months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed part has been present for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.\n\nOr showering, especially with this ankle brace, becomes this whole production. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m circling the same answer, sorry. I don’t know that I can call it a clean “yes.” Dancing is… complicated. It’s work, it’s identity, it’s like asking if I’ve lost interest in having knees—well, bad example, because my knees have opinions.\n\nI still notice movement. I still think about spacing, timing, why someone’s arm looks honest in one version and decorative in another. So there’s some thread still attached. But the pleasure part is harder to find sometimes. It’s not like it used to be where I’d hear music and feel this immediate pull.\n\nOther activities… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t been paying as much attention. I can tell you dancing feels muted, but I’m not sure what box that goes in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions lately, even for small things?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with decisions or trying to finish something that should be straightforward. Like I’ll sit down to work on a grant report, and then a truck goes by outside and I’m listening to that, and somehow I’m thinking about a loading dock at a theater I worked in years ago.\n\nOr I’ll notice a photo on the wall, or one sentence in an old rehearsal note, and suddenly I’m making a completely different plan in my head—like a new piece, or reorganizing the whole rehearsal schedule—when I was supposed to be doing the report. It’s not that I can’t think at all. It’s more that my mind keeps grabbing the next shiny thread before I’ve finished the one in my hand.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had any thoughts about harming yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

I… I don’t know. Not in a way I’ve sat down and made a plan or anything—sorry, that’s probably not exactly what you asked.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F33.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is I’ve been waking up with this heavy, hollow sadness. It’s hard to explain—like the studio lights are on, but there’s no music in me anymore. And I know that sounds dramatic, but I’m a dancer, so that’s kind of how my brain translates things. I keep thinking about this one rehearsal space I used to love, the old floorboards and everything, and now even imagining it just feels… empty.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, and have you had similar periods of sadness before?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. The sad part, I mean. It kind of crept in and then just stayed. I keep trying to mark it by rehearsals or seasons—like, “Was that before the spring showcase?”—because dancers remember time by performances, not calendars, you know? But yes, roughly nine months of feeling this way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or felt restless and unable to sit still, especially when you're alone with your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slower, definitely. Not so much restless. Like recently, even just crossing the studio to pick up my notebook feels weirdly difficult, like my body is moving through wet sand instead of moving with the music. And I’ll notice the room more than I should—the tape marks on the floor, someone’s water bottle by the mirror—and then I’m like, why am I still standing here? I was just trying to get my notebook.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that things you used to enjoy, like dancing or other activities, don’t bring you the same satisfaction anymore?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, dancing is complicated because it’s work and it’s also the thing that used to make everything make sense. Some days I’ll still put on music and think, okay, maybe this will click, and then I get distracted by, like, the angle of the mirrors or whether the speaker is buzzing—there’s this one speaker at the studio that always cuts out on low bass, which drives me crazy.\n\nSo… I don’t know if I’d say it brings no satisfaction. It’s just harder to tell what I’m feeling about it lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to focus or make decisions lately, like during rehearsals or in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, actually. I’ll sit down meaning to do one thing—like finish a grant report—and then it’s like my attention just gets yanked sideways. A truck goes by outside, or I notice a photo on the wall, or I read one phrase in an old rehearsal note and suddenly I’m thinking about a completely different piece I should make, or reorganizing a rehearsal plan, or… I don’t know, following some thread that wasn’t the task at all.\n\nIt’s not that I decide to stop focusing. It just happens, and then I look up and I haven’t finished the thing I sat down to do.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your sadness and difficulty focusing have been present every day, more days than not, for the past year, or has there been more of an on-and-off pattern?\n\nPatient: It’s been… not the whole year, no. I think the low part has been around nine months. Maybe a little hard to pin down exactly, because time gets weird when I’m in rehearsal cycles—like everything is counted by performances or deadlines instead of months—but, yeah, about nine months that it’s been hanging around.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over the past several months—like eating less than usual or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t really track it like a diet thing or anything. When I was more, um, buzzing with ideas, I’d just skip meals and not notice until later. Like I’d realize I’d had coffee and maybe half a banana, and then somehow it was midnight.\n\nAnd now it’s different but kind of the same result—food just tastes like cardboard a lot of the time. Even things I usually like. My rehearsal clothes have been hanging looser, which at first I thought was maybe just the fabric stretching out, because the elastic on dancewear is so unreliable, but… no, I think I’ve lost some weight without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not good enough, even when others seem satisfied with your work or efforts?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community that was supposed to be there. People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.\n\nThere’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.\n\nSo… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. I mean, with dancing, it’s hard to separate “pleasure” from habit and responsibility, because I’ve been doing it so long that even brushing my teeth feels less automatic than warming up sometimes.\n\nThere are moments where I still notice things—like a phrase of music or someone’s weight shift—and part of me responds to it. But whether that’s enjoyment or just my dancer brain doing what it always does, I don’t know. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.\n\nOr showering, especially with this ankle brace, becomes this whole production. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m circling the same answer, sorry. I don’t know that I can call it a clean “yes.” Dancing is… complicated. It’s work, it’s identity, it’s like asking if I’ve lost interest in having knees—well, bad example, because my knees have opinions.\n\nI still notice movement. I can tell you dancing feels muted, but I’m not sure what box that goes in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions lately, even for small things?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with decisions or trying to finish something that should be straightforward. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. I mean, with dancing, it’s hard to separate “pleasure” from habit and responsibility, because I’ve been doing it so long that even brushing my teeth feels less automatic than warming up sometimes.\n\nThere are moments where I still notice things—like a phrase of music or someone’s weight shift—and part of me responds to it. But whether that’s enjoyment or just my dancer brain doing what it always does, I don’t know. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of energy or felt fatigued almost every day, even when you haven’t been physically active?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like “I ran a rehearsal for six hours” tired, because that would make sense. It’s more like I answer one email to a dancer—just one, not even a complicated one—and then I feel like I need to sit down.\n\nOr showering, especially with this ankle brace, becomes this whole production. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m circling the same answer, sorry. I don’t know that I can call it a clean “yes.” Dancing is… complicated. It’s work, it’s identity, it’s like asking if I’ve lost interest in having knees—well, bad example, because my knees have opinions.\n\nI still notice movement. I still think about spacing, timing, why someone’s arm looks honest in one version and decorative in another. So there’s some thread still attached. But the pleasure part is harder to find sometimes. It’s not like it used to be where I’d hear music and feel this immediate pull.\n\nOther activities… I don’t know. I guess I haven’t been paying as much attention. I can tell you dancing feels muted, but I’m not sure what box that goes in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, concentrating, or making decisions lately, even for small things?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially with decisions or trying to finish something that should be straightforward. Like I’ll sit down to work on a grant report, and then a truck goes by outside and I’m listening to that, and somehow I’m thinking about a loading dock at a theater I worked in years ago.\n\nOr I’ll notice a photo on the wall, or one sentence in an old rehearsal note, and suddenly I’m making a completely different plan in my head—like a new piece, or reorganizing the whole rehearsal schedule—when I was supposed to be doing the report. It’s not that I can’t think at all. It’s more that my mind keeps grabbing the next shiny thread before I’ve finished the one in my hand.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. I’m not sure I can answer it cleanly. Dancing isn’t just a hobby for me, so “interest” feels like the wrong drawer to put it in. Some days I still get caught by a piece of music or the way someone crosses a room, and I’ll think about how I’d shape it.

I mean… yes, that one’s been pretty loud.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, like I’m watching bad rehearsal footage and I can’t stop pausing it at all the worst moments. I tell myself I let the dancers down, and the funders, and honestly the whole community that was supposed to be there. People have actually been… kinder than I think I deserve, which almost makes it worse somehow, because then I feel like I’m failing at even accepting that.\n\nAnd I know cancellations happen—venues cancel things, budgets get weird, people get injured, all of that—but in my head it just turns into, “You weren’t good enough to hold it together.”\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuine enjoyment or pleasure in dancing or any other activity—even for a short moment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I keep trying to think of a clean example, like, “Oh yes, that one class” or “that one song,” but then my brain starts sorting it into whether it was actually enjoyment or just… relief that I got through it.\n\nDancing is tricky because it’s also work, you know? Like if a combination lands well, part of me is already thinking about spacing, counts, whether someone’s knee looked safe, whether the music cue is too early—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear moment I can point to right now and say, yes, that was genuine pleasure. Maybe there have been little flashes, but I’m having trouble identifying them.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve completely lost interest in dancing, or do you still feel some connection to it, even if it’s not what it used to be?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say completely lost. That sounds very final, like cutting the music and walking out of the studio forever, and I don’t think it’s that.\n\nThere’s still… something there. Like when I see someone move in a certain way, my brain still sort of catches it and starts arranging shapes, counts, pathways—sorry, that’s a very choreographer answer. It’s just not the same feeling. It used to feel more alive, more like I was being pulled toward it, and now sometimes it feels like I’m standing outside the studio window watching somebody else’s life.\n\nSo… connection, yes, probably. Interest, maybe dimmed way down. Not gone, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer it cleanly. It’s like when you hear your old school’s bell ring in a dream and suddenly you’re late for chemistry class, even though you’re 34 and haven’t thought about chemistry in years—sorry, that’s a weird comparison.\n\nSo… I wouldn’t say I’ve totally lost interest. But I can’t really point to pleasure the way I used to. I start thinking about balance and the towel and not slipping, and then afterward I’m wiped out like I did a full warm-up, which is ridiculous.\n\nAnd lying down doesn’t really fix it. I’ll rest and still feel sort of… drained, like the battery icon says it’s charging but nothing is actually going in.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve experienced a loss of interest or pleasure in dancing or other activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I feel like I’m circling the same answer, sorry. I don’t know that I can call it a clean “yes.” Dancing is… complicated. It’s work, it’s identity, it’s like asking if I’ve lost interest in having knees—well, bad example, because my knees have opinions.\n\nI still notice movement. I still think about spacing, timing, why someone’s arm looks honest in one version and decorative in another. So there’s some thread still attached. But the pleasure part is harder to find sometimes.
